General Church Calendar:

A List of the Principal Changes made
by the Decree of October 28 , 1913 .

Consequent on the precedence now given to the Sunday
Office and Mass (see Note V., p. xii), the feasts hitherto
assigned in the General Church Calendar to certain
Sundays have now been assigned to the following days:

The M.II. Name of Jesus to the Sunday falling on
January 2, 3, 4, or 5; and, such Sunday failing, then to
January 2.

The Solemnity of St. Joseph, to the Wednesday before
the 3rd Sunday after Easter.

The Vigil and the feast of St. John the Baptist’s
Nativity to their old dates of June 23 and 24.

The feast of Our Lord’s Most Precious Blood from the
1st Sunday in July to July 1.

St. Joachim to August 16, vice St. Hyacinth moved
to August 17: St. Laurence’s Octave-day being com¬
memorated on the 17th.

The feast of the VII. Dolours B.V.M., from the 3rd
Sunday in September to September 15.

The feast of tho M.H. Rosary to October 7, but its
popular observance to be on the 1st Sunday in October.

As to Dedication - Anniversaries, see Note VII.,
Clause 2, p. xv.

Feasts Outside the General Calendar.

Excepting where other provision may be made by the
Holy See, the undermentioned dates have been fixed by
Papal Decree of April 28, 1914, for the celebration, when
such celebration is d’lly authorized, of the following
feasts, viz. :

Tho Holy Family, January 19; M.H. Redeemer,
July 15;

M.P. Heart of B.V. Mary, Saturday after the Octave
of Corpus Christi;

B.V. Mary of Perpetual Succour, June 27; Joys of
B.V. Mary, August 27 ;

B V. Mary of Consolation. Saturday after the feast of
St. Augustine, August 28;

Maternity of B.V. Mary, October 11; Purity of
B.V. Mary, October 16.

I. Of the Celebration of Mass generally.

T HE Missal, or Maes-Booh, b the BotA
of the Bites, Ceremonies, and Prayers
prescribed for the celebration of the
Eucharistic Sacrifice of the Body and
Blood of Christ, offered, under the forms of Bread and
Wine, in remembrance of His Passion and Death. This
Sacrifice is the principal act of worship of the Catholio
Church, and, in its Liturgy, U commonly called the Mass,
from the Latin Mista.

Though the term Mass primarily signifies the
J/oe* actual Sacrifice, it is also used in a rubrical
CVj of tAs sense to denote the rite*, ceremonies, and
%k/rd ' prayers with which the Sacrifice is celebrated *
as, for instance, High Mass, Low Mass. In
the Missal, the term Mass more especially denotes incse
portions of the Prayers for Mass which vary from day to
day, as distinguished from those which always remain
unchanged. Thus, the direction in the Missal, * Mass
Protexisti,’ signifies that the varying portions of Mass are
those of which the Introit begins with the word ‘ Protexisti,
being those prescribed lor the feast of a Martyr occurring
in Easter-time. The portions of Mass which never vary
are contained in the ‘ Order ’ or ' Ordinary of the Mass
those which vary according to tho Season or Feast, in the
' Proper oi the Season,' and the * ProjKir ’ or ' Common of
Saints.'

Tho Liturgy contained in tbe Homan Mimed
Tkt Hm is that which, in its essential forms, has from
M\*aai. time immemorial been used by the Itoman
Church. In the earliest ages, the service of
Hass was contained in several books, one of which, the
Jacramentary, developed, about the eighth century, into
the MissaL Eight centuries later, when, in tbe ooorne of
time, the text of the Missals had become considerably cor
rupted, and there was no proper uniformity in tbe celebra¬
tion of Mass, Poj>e 8t Pius V., acting under a decree of the
Council of Trent, caused the text to be carefully oorrectod
and brought back to primitive and approved form*. This

iy NOTES AND DIRECTIONS.

Missal was issued in 1570, and its use strictly enjoined in
all Churches aud Religious Orders unable to show a pre¬
scription of two hundred years for their own Missals.
It was revised and re-issued by Pope Clement VIII. in 1604,
and again, in 1634, by Pope Urban VIIL Many feasts
have since been added, but the Prayers and Rubrics of the
Mass itself remain unchanged. Modifications in the
observance of minor feasts were made by Pope Leo XIII.
in 1882; whilst among the rubrical changes made by Pope
Pius X. in 1911 and 1913 may be specially noted the
restored precedence of the Sunday Office and Mass.

The Roman Missal is now practically used throughout
the Catholic world, although, exceptionally, the Oriental
Churches, the Church of Milan, and one or two Religious
Orders (as, for instance, the Dominicans), continue, by
authorized prescription, to celebrate Mass after their own
Rites.

The Sacrifice of the Mass is the great means of
mr.tagm, obtaining the and blessing of Almighty

and ’ God. When a Priest celebrates Mass, all the
Obligati#*, faithful living and departed share with him in
of Hearing the benefits derived therefrom. But the Priest
Ma ** himself, and those for whom he particularly
prays, as also, in a minor degree, those present
at the Mass, have a special share in these benefits. Hence,
in what is called * saying Mass’ for a person, the Priest so to
speak transfers to that person, in so far as the latter is
competent to receive it, a special share of the graces and
blessings vouchsafed by Almighty God. All Catholics are
bound to assist at Mass on Sundays and on what are termed
Holidays of Obligation : end V'shops and Priests having ths
cure of souls, are bound jn y Mass for their people on

those days, and on others formerly ‘ of obligation, ’ now
called * days of devotion.’—In England and Scotland, and
generally in newly erected Dioceses and in Districts ad-
ministered by Vicars Apostolic, Bishops alone have, pro¬
perly speaking, the core of souls.

In the first ages, Mass was celebrated only
Frt*rp*mey once each day in the same church. In somo
of places, no Mass at all was said on the weekdays
QeUbr* Han. ia Lent; whilst in others, all through the year,
it was said only on Sundays ; or on Sundays,
Saturday*, and the feasts of the Martyrs, I^ater on, in the
West, these limitations disappeared, and it became the
custom, speaking generally, not only for all Priests to
eelebrate Mass daily, but for Priests to celebrate several
times a day, as often indeed as each thought good. Sur

NOTES AND DIRECTIONS.

rivals of the early restrictions remain to this day in the
single Mass said on Maundy Thursday and Holy Saturday,
and the omission of Maas on Good Friday; the subsequent
latitudo being represented by the three Mosses which
Priests may say on Christmas and All Souls' Days.

The present cnBtom is for Priests to say Mass daily,
althougn there is no obligation; but a Priest cannot, for
his private devotion, say more than one Mass each day,
except on Christmas and All Souls' Days. Authority,
however, to ‘duplicate, 1 that is to say two Masses—and
sometimes even to say three Masses—on Sundays and Holi¬
days of Obligation, is given where there is a scarcity of
Priests, and where, if there were no duplication, numbers
of people would be unable to h*ar Maas.

In the first sges there was no fixed time for the

Ho%r <ff celebration of Mass. Later on, the hour, on
CeUbratum. Hundays arid festivals, was after the Office (or
’ Hour ') of Terce, or about 9 a.m. ; on certain
week-days, after Soxt, or soon after mid-day; on fust days,
after None, or about 3 p.m. At present, and for several
centuries back, the hours during which Mass can he cele¬
brated have lx*en limited in tho West (that is, practically, by
those using a Latin Liturgy), to between dawn and mid
day ; and even on days when the Rubrics still direct that
Mass is to be said after Sextor None, Mass is not celebrated
In the afternoon, but those Offices are anticipated instead.

The differences in the ritee and ceremonies
Ditinciimu ssod at Mass, and the circumstances in which
*n tou, A*. Maas is celebrated, give rise to distinction*
which may be classed and described as follows :

(a) Ju v, tlifjh or ole win If a n $ (in Latin, Miss*
Solemnly), the rrietrt (termed the * Celebrant ’) is assisted
by a Deacon and a Subdeacon (whose functions daring
Mass are now generally performed by Prieeta acting in
their stead for the time being); six or more candles art
lighted on the Altar, beside** others borne by acolytes:
incense and musk* are used: portions of the Mass are sung
by the Priest and his assistants, each singly, and other
portions by the e)»oir

(ft) A l on t if icaf Mata is u High Mass ©eb'brnted
by s Pisbop (or a mitred Abbot) with special ceremonies.—
Certain Prelates of the Pope’s Household have the privilege
of occasionally celebrating High Mass very mnch after the
maimer of Hiahops.

(c) A If in a C a n t a t a is in tho nature of a High ¥*<*.

NOTM AND DIRKCrriO^*.

vi

ted to Churches where the Clergy are insufficient, takes tb*
j»laoe of the Missa Solemnis. The Celebrant singe certain
{arts, bat he is not assisted by Deacon or Subdeacon ; and
if incense is used, it is by privilege.

(d) A Low Mast (Musa Privata) ia one the whole o!
which is read, and not sang, by the Celebrant. There is
neither incense, litargical singing, nor any of the solemnities
of High Mass.

( t) The Parochial Matt is the principal Mass,
whether High or Low, on Sundays, in a Parish Church.
Parishioners are exhorted to assist when possible at this
Mass, during which the Parish Notices and Banns of Mar
tiage are usually published, and a sermon is preached.

( f ) The Conventual Mast is the Mass Maid or euiig
in con junction with the Breviary Office, by the Chapter aud
Clergy of a Cathedral or Collegiate Church, or the com¬
munity of a Monastery or Convent. It is said, according to
the Office of the day, after either Terce, Bext, or None, and
is offered for Benefactors. A Capitular Mass is the Con¬
ventual Mass of & Cathedral or Collegiate Chapter.

(g) A Votive Mast is one said, outside the Office of
the day, for a particular purpose, public or private, as, for
instance, for the needs cf the Church, the preservation of
public peace, the safety of travellers, the sick, &c.

(A) A Requiem Mass, bo called from the first word ol
the Introit, is a Mass for the Dead.—(A Dirge, bo called
from the first word of the first Anthem,' Dirige Doinine,’ ia
Hie Office of Matins and Lands for the Demi - also tilled
' Tsgilig),’ the Watches, from being sung at night.)

In the early Church, especially during the per-
?kt Altar, secutions, Mass was said over the tombs of the
Martyrs: the marble slab which covered the
fopulehre serving as the altar-stone. In these days, an
altar at which Mass is celebrated must either be of stona
and contain (in a shrine or otherwise) the relics of Martyrs,
—or, if the altar is not of stone, there must be placed on the
Altar a stone slab containing relics of at least two Martyrs,
and large enough to receive the Host, and most of the
Chalice. The Altar or Slab must be consecrated by a
a Bishop, or by an Abbot empowered by the Holy Bee.
The Altar must be covered with three linen cloths, and on
it must be placed a Cross (Crucifix), with at least ona
candle on each side of it. Under the Cross should 1*) placed
the card of the ‘ Secret ’ prayers ; and cm the Epistle side,
* stand for the Misss !

rti

In the early Church the imn * Confession * came io be
□eod for a Martyr's tomb, or for tho altar over the tomb.
So that now, * St. Peter's Confession ’ mey mean tithes
his tomb or the altar above it in SL Peter s at Borne.

The Vestment* which the Priest uses in the
Ti * celebration of Ma**, and which he puts on ovel

a. his cassock, ore: (a) the Amice, a white linen
cloth, placed first on the bead, and then laid
about the neck and shoulders; (6) the Alb, of white linen,
with sleeves, reaching from head to foot, and gatberod
together at the loins by (c) the Girdle; (d) the Maniple
which hangs over the left arm; (e) the Stolo, which bangs
down from the neck, and is crossed over the breast; and (/)
tke Chasuble, the distinctive vestment for Mass, which
covers the Priest before and behind, as far as the knees.

A Lisbon, in addition to the above, wears other vest-
aeut*; and daring some of the lees solemn portions of
Mass his h*ad is covered by the initre, ami at times he holds
the crosier.

The Deacon and Bnhd. soon are reeled in amice, alb, and
girdle : over these tho Deacon wears the maniple, the stole
from the left nhoulder, and a D&hnAtie in place of the
Chasuble. The Snbdeueon is without stole, and wears the
1 Tuiucella,’ a vestment in these days almost similar to the
deacon's dalmatic. But on the Sundays in Advent and Lent
(excepting Gaudete and Latare l, and on certain week-days,
they either wear folded chasubles in lieu of dalmatics, or
dispense with the outer vestment.

The colour of the maniple, stole, ohapublo and
Oolmmr if (A< dalmatics, »a well as of the veU covering the
fniMtmU. chalice, varies with the day or feast

White ,—more especially the colour of joy, and
origiuaDy the only colour employed,—is used on festivals of
Our Lord other tlian those commemorating llis Passion;
on the Bundays after Esst^r; on the feasts of the B.V
Mary, of Angels, and of Saints who are not Martyrs, and
during the Octaves of these feasts; aftd generally when
another colour is not preecrih'vl.

Bed is used during Whitsuntide • on the feasts commemo¬
rating Oar Lord’s Passion ; and generally on the feasts ni
Apostles and Martyrs, and during their Octavo*.

Purple or Viclet is used on the Sundays and Weekday*
in Advent, and from Bcptuagcsima to Er.ster ; snd, gene¬
rally, on Vigil* and Ember-days, aud during Blessings and
Processions but not in festive Procession.* or thorn of t)\e
Pirated fUarminert

fTGTEJJ aNO DIKrOTIO^s.

riii

Qrtcn im used on the Sundays and Weekdays from it*
Octavo of the Epiphany to Septuagesima Sunday, and from
Trinity Sunday to Advent, ali exclusively; but not when
Mass is of a feast, or during an Octave.

Black is used on Good Friday, and in Masses and Offices
tf the dead.

A candidate for the Priesthood, after proper
Ordination examinations, receives first the Tonsure (becom-
if a Priest. ing then a Cleric); next the four Minor Orders ;

then the Sobdiaconate and Diaconate ; and
lastly, when twenty-five years old. the Priesthood. The
Tonsure and all the Orders are conferred by a Bishop, the
Tonsure being conferred at any place or time; but the
times appointed for ordination to the Major Orders are the
Saturdays in the four Ember-weeks, Saturday before
Passion-Sunday, and Holy Saturday : and these Orders are
conferred publicly, and, if possible, in the CathedraL

A Bishep-Elect, when the necessary authority
Consecration has been received from the Pope, is consecrated
if a Buhap. with great solemnity by at least three Bishops —
one being the consecrator, the others Assistants.
The day chosen is normally a Sunday or the feast of an
Apostle (Bishops being successors of the \ Apostles).—The
Cathedral of a diocese is the Church in which the Bishop
has his throne, or ‘chair’ (Greek, katkedra ).—An Arch¬
bishop receives from the Pope the ‘ Pallium, ’ a circular
trip of wool with pendants, which he wears round the
*eck when celebrating High Mass, but only on certain
lays—speaking generally, the feasts of obligation and
Ihose now of devotion. An Archbishop who has received the
Pallium has the right of haring a Cross borne before him.

The Consecration or Dedication of a Church is
Consecration performed by a Bishop, with the greatest
'v'« ChMrsh. solemnity : a Sunday or feast-day being prefer¬
ably chosen for the ceremony. Among the acts
is Lb* placing of twelve crosses round the Church—three on
4 *cb wall—at a height of about tea feet. Before each cross
K candle burns during the Office# of the wholo Octave of
Dedication, and of the anniversary Octave# thereof.

NOTES AND DIRECTIONS

II Tho Ecclesiastical Year : its Seasons
and Feasts.

1. The Jewish Year was a lunar year of 12

months, or 52 weeks—beginning in March—
Th* Tiar. with compensations to bring it into some har¬
mony with the solar year. Such too was the
old Roman Year, till about 5 Ub.c., when Julius Cmsar sub¬
stituted a solar year of 3f>5 days and 6 hours—tho time taken
by the sun to go through the Zodiac. As these 6 hours in
four years made up one day, it was provided that every
fourth vear should have 366 days. Such a year, ‘leap year,'
was called 1 Bissextile,’ because in it the 24th February, the
1 Sexto Kalendas Martii,' or 6th day before March of tho
Roman Calendar, was twico repeated. But, as in reality
the solar year consists of only 365 days, 5 hours and 48
minutes, the addition of one day every four years was more
than tho compensation required, and, in the course of sixteen
ceuturies, resulted in the Calendar Equinoxes being ten days
behind their actual occnrrence. To rectify this, Rope Gre-

f jory XIII. directed (11 that in 1582 October 4th should be
mmediately followed by the 15th; and (2) that thenceforth
certain years which had hitherto been leap years should cease
to bo so. This reform was not adopted iu England till 1752;
whilst the Greeks and Russians still keep to tne ‘ Old Style.’

The Ecclesiastical Year begins with the First
. . . . .Sunday of Advent—the Sunday nearest the

C TtX. Ual °"* St. Andrew the Apostle (30 November).

It may be divided into tho ‘ Seasons ’ of Advent,
Christmas, Epiphany, After - Epiphany, Septuagesima,
Lent, Passion tide, Easter, Pentecost or Whitsuntide, and
After-Pentecost. In addition to, aad independent of, those
Seasons and tbs festivals connected with them, thcro are
many feasts commemorative of Our Lord and the Blessed
Virgin Mary, a« also the almost daily feasts of Martyrs
and other Saints.

Of the Festivals and Seasons, some are inove-
MortaoU able, others fixed. The Moveable Feasts, pro-
and perly »o called, depend upon Easlvr-Day, which,
Pix*i Ftaiu, by a decree of tho Niceno Council (a.d. 325),
must be celebrated 'on the Lord’s Day first
following the fourteenth Moon of the First Month.’—in
other words, on the first Sunday afttr the full Moon which
happens on or after the 21st day of March. A Table, show¬
ing the dates on which the principal Moveable Roasts occur

III. Classification, <£c., of Sundays and Feasts.

In respect of classification,

Sundays are classed as greater (‘ major’) of the 1st and
2nd class, and lesser minor ’) or ordinary Sundays.

F e r i a r, as ‘ major * or ‘ minor ’: the major being sub'
divided into privileged and non-privileged.

Feasts, as doubles, semi-doubles, or simples; the
doubles being subdivided into 1st class, 2nd class,
greater, and lesser.

A table showing the precedence of Sundays and feasts
will be found on p. xviie.

Doubles They are so called from the doubling of the An-
6 * theme on these feasts : that is, from the Anthems
being sung or said in their entirety before as well as after the
Psalms and Canticles of Matins, Lauds, and Vespers ; whilst
on Semi-doubles and Simples the first words only of the An¬
thems arc said before, though the whole is said after, the Psalms
and Canticles.

Octaves Some of the greater feasts are observed (a) cither

c 8 * during eight consecutive days, or (6) only on the

first and eighth day. This observance is in either case termed
an Octave, the first and last days being specially observed. The
middle days, or days from the second to the seventh inclusive,
arc termed 1 days within the Octave’ (infra Octavam). Note IX.,
p. xvii.

p . Liturgically, ‘ Feria ’ has several meanings : (a)

* 6 r ‘festum feriatum,’ a holidaying-feast, a day on

which there is the obligation of abstaining from servile work
and of hearing Mass; ( b ) ‘ Ferhe,* the seven days of the week,
beginning with Sunday as ‘ feria prima,’ and so on to Saturday
as 'feria septima’; but Sunday is always called 'Dominica/ the
Lord’s Day, and Saturday, 'Sabbatum,’ the Sabbath; ( c ) in the
Breviary and Missal, ‘feria'is the equivalent of the English ‘week¬
day ’ as opposed to Sunday, and likewise the day on which the
Office and Mass are neither of a Sunday, Feast, Octave, or Vigil.

a f a +1 « . Before the Mass of certain Sundays, feasts, and

at non*, week-days, a note is placed stating that the
* Station ’ is at such or such a church. The explanation is
that from an early age it was the custom in Rome to visit
processionally tho thrines and churches connected with the
Martyrs. Such visits were called Stations; and the days and

churches now marked in the liiosal as Stations are those finally
fixed by Popo St. Gregory the Great ( a.d. 590 to 004). The pro-
cession, 4cc., has only been suspended since 1870 ; but otherwise
this celebration is kept up in the traditional way.

IV. The ’ Ordering ’ of Mass.

Normally, Mass, on any given day, should
}• , . ,.agree with the office of the Breviary, and be
t ^ r °* cither (a) of the Season, or (L) of tho feast

2. By * Mass of tho Season,’ or * Mass of the Sunday’
or ‘Foria’ (».«., week-day), is meant the proper or
common Mass which, in that portion of the Missal called
‘the Proper of tho Season,’ is, day by day, provided for
all the Sundays and week-days of tho year. By ‘ Mass
of the Feast ’ or ' Saint ’ is meant the proper or common
Mass provided in the ‘Proper’ or ‘Common of Saints’
for tho fixed feasts shewn in the Calendar.

It, however, constantly happens that there

relative rank or class of tho days clashing.

4. Speaking generally, when two feasts—and here the
term foast includes Sundays, Feasts, Ferias, Vigils,
dec. —fall on the same day, the Mass said is that of the
feast which is higher in rank ; tho other feast is either
(a) transferred, (h) commemorated, or (c) altogether
omitted,suppressed. Thus, if superseded,

a. Pouhles of tho 1st and 2nd class aro transferred.

b. Sundays and majnr-fcrlas aro always commemorated.

b or c. Greater and leaser doubles, semi-doubles, octavos,
vigils, simnles, and ferias, are either commemorated or
suppressed, according to tho dignity of the feast which
is taking precedence.

The Mass to be said on a Sunday is the

1. Precedence. ' Dominical Mass,’ the Mass of the Lord’s

VI. Week-days.

1. Speaking generally. Mass on a week-day will be
that of the feast entered in the General or local Calendar
as occurring on that date, but when such a feast clashes
with some movable feast or privileged day, one or other
of the clashing feasts is either transferred, commemorated,
or suppressed, as explained in Note VII., Clauses 4 to 8,
p. xvi, and Tables, pp. xviie, xvii/, and xvii#.

VIII. Conventual Masses.

In Cathedral and Collegiate, as also In

1. Cathedral t ]\j onaa tic and other Conventual Churches

tome where there is the obligation on the Clergy

are said : one, after Terce, of the feast, the others

Rogation, and Christmas days; (6) on the following

of Advent, Lent, and Easter, there is said after

IX. Octave8. (See the Note, p. x.)

Octaves arc either privileged, common, or simple—

1. The privileged are of three 4 Order* ’:

(a) 1st Order.—Easter and Pentecost. Mass is
always of the Octave. 1st and 2nd class doubles, which
may come to fall on days of the Octavo, are transferred
to after the Octave ; feasts of lower rank are suppressed
if falling on the Sunday, Monday, or Tuesday, and com¬
memorated if falling on the remaining days.

(b) 2nd Order.—E p i p h a n y and Corpus Christ i.
Mass is always of the Octave, unless there fall on any of
the middle days any 1st class double ; or, on the last
day, a 1st class double binding on the whole Church : in
either of which cases the Mass is of such feast,—but the
Octave must always be commemorated; 2nd class doubles

NOTES AND DIRECTIONS.

XVllrt

are transferred to after the Octave, and feasts of lowor
rank are commemorated.

(c) 3rd Order .—C hristmas and Ascension. Mass
is of the Octave unless when a double or semi-don bio
feast falls on anv of the middle days, or a 1st or ‘2nd class
double falls on the last day : in which case Mass is of the
feast, but always with a commemoration of the Octave.

2. Common Octaves are the Octaves of such
non-privileged, or local, or secondary 1st class
doubles as may have an Octave attached. The middle
days give way to doubles and semi-doubles, the Octave
being commemorated on all but superseding 1st and ‘2nd
class doubles. The last day, if superseded by a 1st or
‘2nd class double, is commemorated, provided such feast
is not a 1st class double feast of our Lord binding on tho
whole Church.

8. Simple Octaves are the Octavos of such doublos
of the 2nd class as have an Octavo attached, but
there is no observance of the middle days; whilst the
Octave-day ranks only as u simple, though taking pre¬
cedence of other simples.

It sometimes happens that Patronal and

4, not similar feasts having an Octave, fall upon
tr*$nferabU. privileged days (p. xvii«, a, b, c, f), and have

therefore to ne transferred. In such cases
the Octave is not transferred with tho feast, but is either
partially or totally omitted. It is totally omitted when
the feast is transferred to, or beyond, the day which would
in the normal course have been the hist day of its Octave.
It is partially omitted when the feast is transferred to a
day within the Octave, in which case only the remaining
days of the Octavo are observed.

5. Moreover, such days of any Patronal or similai
Octave as fall between December 17 snd ‘2,'j, between

Ash Wednesday and Low Sunday, or Wbitsun-eve aud
Trinity Sunday, inclusively, are suppressed.

* X. Mosses for tho Dead.

Th* Sacrifice of the Mass may at all times l>o speciallj
offered for the Faithful Departed: but the singing or
saying of Itequirm (‘ lilock ’) Masses, is governed ny rules
of which tho principal may bo summarized as follows

xriib notes and directions.

1. On November 2, A11 8 o u 1 s’ D a y. As to its observ¬
ance and possible transfer, see ‘The Proper of Saints,' Novem¬
ber 2. Neither Breviary Office nor Mass other than for the
Dead is said on All Souls’ Day : which, rubrically, ends after
Vone and High Mass.

2. On the day of death, or day to count as such.
Requiem Masses may be sung or said, but not if the day be a
Sunday, a holiday of obligation, a 1st or 2nd class feast, ora feria
which excludes 1st class feasts ; or if the Blessed Sacrament be
exposed.

3. When the Body of the Deceased is present
in Church. A Requiem Mass may be sung: but not if the
day be one of the principal feasts (marked * on page xvib), or
if the Blessed Sacrament be solemnly exposed. Where, however,
owing to poverty, or insufficient Clergy and Canters, a sung
Mass is not possible, a Low Mass may be said instead.

4. On the 3rd, 7th, 30th (‘Month’s Mind') day after
death or burial, and on Anniversaries. A Requiem Mans
may be sung : but not if the day be a Sunday or holiday, a day
included in clauses a to g of the Table, p. xviir, within a
privileged Octave (Not* IX.); nor If the Blessad Sacrament is
solemnly exposed.

5. But in Parish Churches where one Mass only is celebrated,
a Requiem Mass cannot be sung or said on any holiday or day
of devotion on which the Parish Priest is bound to say Mass for
his people ; nor on Ash Wednesday, Whitsun Eve if the Font is
blessed, or 8t. Mark’s and the Rogations if there is a Procession.

0. ‘Common’ or Ordinary Requiem Masses may
be sung or said on semi-double and simple feasts : but not on
Sundays nor during the privileged Octaves ; nor during Lent
(except as in Clause 8), nor on an Ember-day, a Vigil, or
Rogation Monday; nor on the Saturday or other week-day on
which an omitted or superseded Sunday’s Mass should be said
(Note V., Clauses 6 and 7); nor when the Blessed Sacrament is
exposed..

7. A Priest, however, can secure the indulgences of the
‘ Privileged Altar ’ by saying the Lenten or other aforesaid ferial
Mass, and inserting, before the last of the Collects for the day,
a Collect for the Dead for whom the Mass is being applied (with
corresponding Hecret and Post-Communion).

8. During Lent a Low Requiem Mass is sanctioned on the
first day in each week which, in the Calendar of the particului
Church, is not occupied by a double feast.

prohibited not only on the aLove days, but also on

PRECEDENCE OY FEaSTS—1.

In addition to the division of the feasts referred to in Note* II
and III, pegea ix, x—namely, the division of feasts according tc
‘ rank ’ (Latin, ritus), as doubles, semi-doubles, and simples, a
further distinction has been made by separating double and
semi-double feasts into ‘primary’ and ‘Beoondary.’

Of the primary 1st olass doable 8, first in order of
precedence come those which are common to, and obligatory on,
the whole (or ‘ Universal’) Church; and next, those especially
solemn for a particular church, parish, diocese, locality, or
religious order—namely (a) a Church’s Dedication, and its Anni¬
versary ; (//) the Anniversary of the Dedication of the diocesan
Cathedral ; (c) a Church's Titular feast; (d) the Titular of the
diocesan Cathedral; (e) the Principal Patron of a town, diocese,
province, or country ; (j) the Titular or Founder of a Religious
Order or Congregation. These foasts are comprised in the
groups a, b, c, d, Table, pago xvlie.

Of other 1st class doubles, that of the Sacred Heart of .Testis is

secondary.

Of the 2nd class doubles entered in the General
Calendar, the following are P r 1 na a r y—viz., the Circumcision,
Transfiguration, Dedication of St. Saviour’s ; the Purification,
Visitation, and Nativity B.V.M.; St. Michael’s Dedication; the
feasts of the 11 Apostles; St. Mark, St. Luke, St. Stephen,
H. Innocents, St. Laurence, St. Joachim, St. Anne.—The other
2nd class doubles are Secondary.

Of the greater doables, the following are Primary

—viz., the Oetave-day of every primary 1st class double; the
Dedications of St. Peter’s .Mid of St. Paul’s, and of 8t. Mary of
the Snows ; the Presentation B.V.M., ; the Guardian Angels ; the
Beheading of St. John Baptist; St. Barnabas, St. Benedict,
St Dominic, St. Francis A sijsi, St. Francis Xavier ; a Minor (».«.,
Secondary) Patron —: 1 i greater doubles are Secondary.

Of lesser doubles and semi-doubles, the ‘natal’
day of a Saint is a primary feast; uad practically every
Saint’s day is either the actual ‘ natal-day ’ (i.e., the day of death,
and therefore of 1 birth 1 to heaven), or the day cotinting as such.
But the feasts of St. Francis’s Stigmata, of St. Stephen’s Find-
ing, and the Octave-day of a secondary 2nd class double are
secondary. So likewise those feasts both of Our Lord and of
the B.V.Mary under some particular Title; and of the Saints
hither than their ‘ natal-day ’) under a Title such as the Finding,
Translation, Patronage, and the like.

These rules apply generally to the feasts which are special to
% country, locality, religious order, <kc.

The Tables on pages xvii* and xvi^f show tho precedence o#
the feasts and days of the Ecclesiastical Year.

Privileged end other days.

cepted) is put buck to the preceding Saturday.—A Vigil falling

pressed.—The Gospel of a Vigil commemorated in Lent, on an

boo page xviid.

A LMIGHTY and everlasting God grant us an increase o{

Let us sorrowfully confess our sins, and resolve to amend.

0 GOD of infinite goodness, how little have we served
Thee in the past: how greatly have we sinned against
Thee! We are heartily sorry tlyit wc have lost that time
in offending Thee which Tlnui gavest ns to he employed in
Thy service, in advancing the good of onr souls, and in ob¬
taining everlasting life. We are sorry above all things that
we have offended Thee because Thou art infinitely good, and
sin is infinitely displeasing to Thee. We desire to love Thee
with our whole hearts, and firmly purpose, by the help of Thy
grace, to serve Thee more faithfully for the time to come.

Let us mentally unite in tlie Saerifico of the Mas.-,

and enjoy Thee for ever in Thy ^lory. Amen.

Let ua place ourselves under the protection of Almighty Ood.
rilHOU knowest, O God, how weak and unablo we are to
I do good. Leave us not to ourselves, bnt take us under

cure the corruption of our hearts; grant that, overcoming

A LMIGHTY and everlasting God, O Lord, Heavenly
Father ; look down upon us, we beseech Thee, with the
eyes of Thine unspeakable goodness, and graciously hearkeu
to the prayers of Thy people. Have merey upou all Chris¬
tians, for whom Thine only-begotten Son, our Lord and
Saviour Jesus Christ, delivered Himself into the hands of
sinners, and of His free will underwent the bitter death of
Ihe Cross. Through Him turn aside, O most graciom
Father, the well-de served punishments of present and im¬
pending evils: ced'tion, war, famine, pestilence, and the
miseries of calamitous times. Enlighten also, and strengthen
in all good, our spiritual and temporal Rulers, that they
may further that which is conducive to Thy divine honour,
our salvation, and the general peace and welfare of the
Christian world. Vouchsafe to us, O God of peace, a right
union in faith, without difference or division. Convert our
hearts to a true repentance, and to a real amendment of
our lives. Kindle in us the fire of Thy loye, and give us an
ardent zeal for all good; that, as loving and obedient
children, we may in life and in death, be ever pleasing and
acceptable to Thee. We also pray, O Lord, as Thou
wiliest that we should pray, for friends and foes; for the
healthy and the sick; for the afflicted ; for those in nee<
and in danger ; for the living and the departed. To Thee,
O Lord, we commend our actions and our omissions; our
undertakings and transactions; our lives and our deaths.
Grant us here the gift of Thy grace; and there make us
one day with all Thine Elect to arrive, where, in perfect
bliss, we may praise and glorify Thee for ever. This ir
mercy grunt us, 0 Heavenly Father, through Jesus Christ
Fliy well-beloved Son ; who with Thee and the Holy Ghost
Jiveth and reigneth one Gcd world without end. Amen.

5V Preparation.

/ ‘OME to mo all ye that labour and are heavy-laden, and
" I will refresh you.—The Bread which I will give, is my
tfoeh for tho life of the world.—Take ye and eat: This is my
Body which shall be delivered for you: this do in memory of
me.—lie that eateth my Flesh, and drinketh my Blood,
abideth in me, and I in him.

T HESE, O Lord, are Thy words; and words of so great
a tenderness, so full of sweetness and love, encourage
me, when my sins and mine unclean conscience would keep
mo back from approaching to so great a Sacrament. The
sweetness of Thy words giveth ine hope, when the multitude
of mine offencos weigheth me down.

Thou commandest me to approach with confidence, if I
would have part with Thee ; and to receive the food of im¬
mortality, if 1 desire to have life and glory everlasting.
Come to me, Thou sayest, all ye that labour and aro heavy-
laden, and I will refresh you.

0 sweet and welcome words in the ear of a sinner, that
Thou, our Lord and God, sliouldest invite the poor and
needy to the Communion of Thy most sacred Body 1
But who am I, Lord, that I should presumo to come to
Thee ? Behold the heaven of heavens cannot contain Thee,
-and Thou sayest, Come ye all to me 1 'What menueth this
most loving condescension, this most friendly invitation ?
How shall I dare to approach Thee ? 1, who am conscious
of no good on which I could presume,—how shall I dare to
introduce Thee into my house ?

Had I, at least, like so many of Thy servants, unceasingly
laboured to please Thee t Had I nover ceased to lament
my ingratitude in corresponding so unworthily to Thine
infinite love and goodness ! But, alas, how little is it that
I do 1 With what little sorrow do I ever grieve for my past
offences I With what apathy do I now continue in Thy
service! How feebly resolved to amend ; how little inclined
to resist temptation I Nay, alas, how much readier perhaps
to court the first impulses to sin !

And yet do Thou, O Good Jesu, vouchsafe to give me,
though so unworthy, some little of Thine all-powerful grace,
that I may confess and bewail these and other my defects
with a hearty sorrow, and a great dislike of mine owu weak-

3

BEFORE HOLY COMMUNION.

M

ness ; that I may make stronger, and yet stronger resolu
tions always to amend my life and to advance in virtue
tkjjt I may endeavour with my whole heart and will to offer
myself as a perpetual holocaust to Thine honour, and faith-
J ully commit to Thee both my body and soul.

And what oblation more worthy, or satisfaction great<t
for the washing away of my sins, can I make, than the offer¬
ing of myself purely and entirely to God, together with the
Oblation of Thy Body and Blood in the Mass and in this
holy Communion ?

Thanks, then, be to Thee, 0 Good Jesu, Eternal Shep¬
herd, who hast vouchsafed to feed us, poor exiles, with Thy
precious Body and Blood; and to invite ns to the receiving
of these Mysteries with the words of Thine own mouth
saying, Come to me all you that labour and are heavy-laden
and I will refresh you.

V - rayer for grace, a firm faith, and the supplying, by Chris'
Himself, of whatever is wanting in ns.
T)KEYENT Thy servant in the blessings of Thy sweet-
L ness, O Lord my God, that I may approach worthily
and devoutly to Thy magnificent Sacrament. Raise up my
heart towards Thee, and free it from all sluggishness. Visit
me witn Thy grace, that 1 may in spiri' taste Thy sweet¬
ness, which so plentifully lies hidden in this Sacrament
Enlighten also mine eyes to behold so great a mystery, and
strengthen me to believe it with an undoubting faith. Foi
it is Thy work, not the power of man ; Thy sacred institu¬
tion, not man’s invention. For no man can be found able
of himself to know and understand these things, which
surpass even the subtlety 01 Angels. How, then, shall I, a
miserable sinner, who am but dust and ashes, be able to
search into, or conceive of so high and sacred a mystery ?

O Lord, in the simplicity of my heart, with a good and
firm faith, and in obedience to Thy command, I come tc
Thee with hope and reverence ; and I do verily believe that
Thou art here present in this Sacrament, God and Man.
And since it is Thy will that I should receive Thee, and
through love imite myself to Thee, I implore Thy mercy,
and beg of Thee to give me for this a special grace ; that
I may be wholly melted away in Thee and may overflow
with Thy love, and may no more seek comfort from any¬
thing else.

And if I cannot as yet be all heavenly and all on fire like
the Cherubim and Seraphim, yet will I endeavour so to
apply myself to devotion, and so to prepare my heart, that

A FI ER HOLY OOMMV.NION,

Mi

humbly receiving tliis Life-giving Sacrament, i may nt
east acquire some small flame of Thy divine lire.

And whatever is wanting in me, do Thou, O Good Jtsu,
most merciful Saviour, in Thy bounty and goodness supply
for me: Thou who hast vouchsafed to call us all to Thee,
saying, Come to me, all you that labour and are heavy-
iulen, and I will refresh you. Do Thou, who hast prepared
Thy Body and Blood for my meat and drink, receive me for
lln* praise and glory of Thy Name ; and grant that, with
the frequenting of this Thy Sacrament, the earnestness of
niy devotion may steadfastly increase. Amen.

\ LMIGIITV and everlasting God, behold I draw near to
the Sacrament of Tliinc only-begotten Son our I^ord
Jesus Christ; and I come, as one sick, to the Physician of
life ; miclenu, to the Fountain of mercy ; blind, to the Light
of eternal glory; poor and needy, to the Lord of heaven
and earth. And I ask, that, in the abundance of Tliinc in¬
finite goodness, Thon wouldest vouchsafe to heal mine infir¬
mity, wash mine uncleanness, enlighten my blindness, enrich
iny ]»overty, and clothe my nakedness : that I may receive
Jie Bread of Angels, the King of kings and the Lord of
lords with such reverence and humility, such contrition and
devotion, such piety and faith, such purpose and intention,
as will most conduce to the salvation of my soul. Grant
me to receive, I beseech Thee, not only the Sacrament of
the Body ami Blood of tin* Lord, hut also the reality and
the virtue of the Sarmment. <) most gracious God, grant
that 1 may so receive the Body of Thine only-begotten Son
our Lord Jesus Christ which He took from the Virgin Mary,
as to attain to he incorjxirated into His mystical body, and
numbered among its members. O most luring Father,
grant that I may one day see face to face and for evermore,
Him, whom now veiled, 1, a pilgrim, purpose to receive on
my way, and who with Thee liveth and reigneth in the
unity of the Holy Ghost God, world without end. Ainen.

Q ^h.tnkogifcing after ^jolo Communion.

1 G1VE Time thanks, Holy Lord, Father Almighty, Ever
lasting God, who without any merit of mine, but in the
pure condescension of Thy mercy, hast vouchsafed to feed me,
Thine unworthy servant, with the previous Body njnl Blood
of Thy Son our Ix>rd Jesus Christ. And J humbly entreat
Tluv (.hut this hoh Communion may not be to me a cause

AFTER UOLY COMMUNION.

iTfii

for punishment, lmt au intercession for pardon. May it be
to me the armour of faith, and the shield of good will: the
blunting and extermination of all vices and evil desires ; an
ncreaso of charity and patience, of humility and obedi¬
ence, and of every virtue ; a sure defence against the snares
of all enemies, visible and invisible ; a quieting of all undue
cravings of mind and body; and a cleaving to Thee, the
true and only God; and the happy consummation of my
end. And I pray that Thou wouldest vouchsafe to bring
me, a sinner, to that unspeakable Banquet, wherein with
Thy Bon and the Holy Ghost, Thou art to Thy Saints true
light, full contentment, everlasting joy, and perfect happi¬
ness. Through the same Christ our Lord. Amen.

W HO will give me, 0 Lord, to find Thee alone, that I may
open my whole heart to Thee, and enjoy Thee as
my soul desireth : that nothing created may affect me any
longer, but that Thou alone mayest speak to me, and I to
Thee, as one that loves is wont to speak to him whom la
loveth, and he that is loved is wont to respond ? This I de¬
sire, tliis I pray for, that I may be wholly united to Thee
and may withdraw my heart from all created tlrnigs, and by
a holy Communion with Thee more and more learn to relish
heavenly and eternal things.

When shall I, O Lord, be wholly united and absorbed h
Tliee, and altogether forgetful of myselL? Thou in me, and
l in Thee; and so grant us both to continue in one. Verily,
Thou art the Choicest among thousands, in whom my soul
Is well pleased to dwell all the days of her life. Verily
Thou art He in whom is sovereign peace and true rest: out
of whom is labour and sorrow, and endless misery. Thou
art in truth the hidden God, whose counsel is not with tin
wicked, but with the simple and the humble.

But what return can I make for this inestimable Gift ?
Behold, nothing will please Thee better than if I give my¬
self up wholly to Thee, and unite myself closely with Thee.
Then will all that is within me rejoice exceedingly ; then
wilt Thou say to me, If thou wilt he with me, I will be with
thee ; and I will answ er, Vouchsafe, O Lord to remain w ith
me, and I will w illingly he with Thee ; for my one desire is
to he wildly united to Thee.

0 SWEETEST Lord Jesu, how great a sweetness have
they, who, with a true ard sincere devotion, feast with
Thee in this Banquet, where Thou alone art set before them:

nOLV COMM l-MON.

Thou who art more to be desired than all the desires of the
heart ! Anil to me it would indeed be delightful to pour ont
my soul in Thy presence, aud with the whole affection of my
heart, like the devout Magdale* , to wash Tliy feet with my
tears.

lint where is this devotion? Where this shedding ol
tears ? Surely in Thy sight, and in that of Thy holy Angels,
my whole 1 ing ought to be inflamed with love, and weep
for joy ? J 'or I have Thee truly present in this Sacrament,
though hidden under another tonn : since to behold Thee
in Thine own divine brightness, is what mine eyes could not
endure, nor could the whole world subsist in the splendour
fu the glory of Thy Majesty.

And since it is out of condescension to my weakness that
Tlam art hidden under the forms of this Sacrament, my de¬
votion ought not to be less lively, if in them I lack the visi¬
ble manifestation of Thy presence. O for the ardent faith
of those who truly know' their Lord in the breaking of bread,
whose heart burns when Jesus is with them ! Alas, such
uffeetio'i, such love ami fervour, are often far from me !

Bui oe Thou merciful to me, () Good Jesii, sweet and
loving Saviour : nml vouchsafe that I, Thy poor and needy
servant, may sometimes, at h ast, feel in Thy Holy Com¬
munion some little of this cordial affection : that so liiy faith
may Ik; strengthened and my hope in Thy goodness in-
leased: aud that my love being once perfectly kindled, and
having tasted the Manna of heaven, it may never again
decay.

tfOST sweet and loving lord, Thou knowest my weak*
Jl ness: by what evils 1 ain surrounded ; how often I am
•ppressand trimhled ; how often tempted and overcome.
To Thee I turn for remedy, to Thee for help and comfort;
to Thee I speak, who knowest all things, to whom all within
me is manifest, and who alone canst perfectly help and com¬
fort me. Thou knowest the things of winch I stand most iu
need, uiul how noor 1 am in virtues.

Behold, before Thee 1 stand, poor and naked, begging Thy
mercy, imploring Thy grace. Do 'Thou, therefore, iutiame
my coldness with the fire of Thy love ; my blindness en¬
lighten with the 1/rightnewB of Thy presence. Teach me to
look all earthly things with indifference, and for Thy
sake to hear with perfect patience all things grievous and
•fllicting. Suffer me not any more to waiide r upon earth,
but lilt up my heart to Thee in heaven. Be Thou my only

AFTER HOLY COIIM'JNION.

sweetness from henceforth and for evermore; for Theft only
Hi*t my meat and my drink, my love and my joy, my delight
and my full contentment.

0 that with Thy presence Thon wonldest inflame and
transform me into Thyself, that I might be made one spirit
with Thee by the grace of internal union, and by the melt¬
ing of an ardent love. Suffer me not, I beseech Thee, to go
away hungry and tliirsty, but deal with me in Thy mercy,
as Thon hast often dealt with Thy Saints. And what mar¬
vel, if I should be w holly set on fire by Thee, and should die
to myself, since Thou art a lire alw'ays burning and never
decaying, a love purifying the heart and enlightening the
understanding.

0 GOD, my everlasting love, my w hole good, and never-
ending happiness, gladly would I receive Thee with the
most vehement desire and most worthy reverence which any
of the Saints have ever had, or could possibly experience.
And although I am unworthy to have all these feeling* Of
devotion, yet I offer Thee the whole affection of my hc a rt
as if I alone had them all. Yea, and whatsoever a p .uis
mind can conceive and desire, all this with the grer test
reverence and most inward affection, I desire to offer and
lay before Thee. Nothing do I wish to keep back , but
freely and most willingly do I offer Thee myself awd all
that is mine.

Vouchsafe, then, to receive, 0 Lord God, this my inten¬
tion and desire of giving Thee infinite praise and blessing,
which according to the greatness of Thine unspeakable
Majesty are most justly due to Thee. These I render, and
desire to render, every day and every moment of my life;
and with my prayers I invite and entreat all the heavenly
Spirits and all the faithful on earth, to join with me in
giving Thee praise and thanks unceasing.

Let all peoples, and tribes, and tongues praise Thee, and
magnify Thy great and holy Name ! And may all who reve¬
rently and devoutly celebrate this Thy most adorable Sacra¬
ment, and receive It with faith, find grace and mercy at Thy
hands, and charitably pray for me, an unworthy sinner.
And may those, who in heaven have obtained their desired
end, and are now happily and gloriously united with Thee,
vouchsafe to cast a look of pity on me, a poor and needy sup¬
pliant, and remember me before the Throne of Thy grace
Amen.

TI7E give Thee thanks, Father Almighty, that in Thy great
VV goodness Thou hast made us, Thine unworthy ser¬
vants, partakers in this most holy Sacrifice, wherein we

Additional Collects.

On feasts of, and above , the rank of 'double,' one Collect only it send,
unless a ' commemoration' has to be ynatle of a Sum Lay, or 'ferial
or of a feast occurring on the same day. On Sundays within an
Octave, tiro Collects are said; on ordinary Sundays and'semi
doubles,' three; on ' simples * and ' feriasthree, five, or even seven.
The 2nd and 3rd Collects to be thus said, vary with the season of
the year, aiul are gitm below.

Collects for a special purpose may be added to the appointed Col¬
lect or Collects, on all days. Doubles of the 1st class, Ac., excepted.

When, on a Sunday, the Mass of a feast us said instead of the
Sunday Mass, the Sunday is commemorated. And similarly, a
minor feast, a week-day In Advent or I^ent, an Ember or Rogation
day, or a day within an Octave , is commemorated when superseded
by a notherfeast. See jyaragraph 6, page xii, and the Table,page xvi

When, on a Sunday or Semi-double., arte commemoration has been
made, the 2ml of the Collects ‘ for the Season ’ is omitted ; and when
l»co commemorations have been made., both those Collects are omitted.

Gospel, Luke 4 T that time, Jesus said to His disciples,
c. 2i,2o-33. j\ There shall be signs in the sun, and in
the moon, and in the stars : and upon earth distress of
nations, by reason of the confusion of the roaring of the
sea and of the waves : men withering away for fear, anti
expectation of what shall come upon the whole world.
For the powers of heaven shall be moved ; and then
shall they see the Sou of Man coming in a cloud with
great power and majesty. But when these things begin
to come to pass, look up, and lift up your heads, because
your redemption is at hand. And He spoke to them
a similitude, See the fig-tree and all the trees ; when
they now shoot forth their fruit, you know that sum¬
mer is nigh. So you also, when you see these things
come to pass, know that the kingdom of God is at
hand. Amen I say to you, this generation shall not
pass away till all be fulfilled. Heaven and earth shall
pass away : but my words shall not pass away.

Gasp., John i T that time, the Jews sent from Jem*
c. 1, 19-2s. sa | ein Priests and Levites to John, to

THE PROPER OF THE SEASON.

38

answered, No. They said therefore unto him, Who
art thou ? that we may give an answer to them that
sent us. What sayest thou of thyself ? He said, I
am the voice of one crying in the wilderness. Make
straight the way of the Lord, as said the Prophet
Isaias. And they that were sent, were of the Phari¬
sees. And they asked him, and said to him, Why
then baptizest thou, if thou be not the Christ, nor
Elias, nor the Prophet ? John answered them, saying,

I baptize with water; but there hath stood one in the
midst of you, whom you know not; He it is, who
shall come after me, who is preferred before me ; the
latchet of whose shoe I am not worthy to unloose.
These things were done in Bethania beyond the Jordan,
where John was baptizing.

of the blind be opened, and the cars of the deaf be
unstopped. Then shall the lame man leap as a hart,
and the tongue of the dumb be free : for waters are
broken out in the desert, and streams in the wilderness.
And that which was dry land shall become a pool, and
the thirsty land springs of water, saith the Lord
Almighty.

Epht., Acts those days, Stephen, full of grace and
c ; £* 1 fortitude, did great wonders and signs

*’ ’ among the people. Now there arose some

of that synagogue which is called of the Libertines,
*nd Cyrenians, and Alexandrians, and of them that
were of Cilicia and Asia, disputing with Stephen ;
and they were not able to resist the Wisdom and the
Spirit that spoke. And hearing these things, they
were cut to the heart, and they gnashed with their
teeth at him. And when Stephen was full of the Holy
Ghost, looking up into heaven, he saw the glory of God,
and Jesus standing at the right hand of God. And he
said, Behold I sec the heavens opened, and the Son of
Man standing on the right hand of God. And they
crying out with a loud voice, stopped their ears, and
with one accord ran violently upon him. And casting
him out of the city, they stoned him ; and the wit*
nesses laid their garments at the feet of a young
man, whose name was Saul. And they stoned Stephen
invoking, and saying, Lord Jesus, receive my spirit.
And falling on his knees, he cried with a loud voice,
saying, Lord, lay not this sin to their charge. And
when he had said this, he fell asleep in the Lord.
